<a id='question-080'></a>

### Question 080: Sarcopenia and Muscle Fiber Type Atrophy
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Age-related sarcopenia involves preferential loss of Type II (fast-twitch) muscle fibers, leading to decreased strength and power, while Type I (slow-twitch) fibers are relatively spared. (年龄相关性肌少症涉及快肌纤维（II型）的优先丧失和萎缩，导致力量和爆发力下降，而慢肌纤维（I型）相对保留。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
An 82-year-old female presents to her primary care physician complaining of increasing difficulty with daily activities. She reports progressive weakness, particularly when climbing stairs or rising from a seated position. Her gait has slowed significantly, and she now walks at a pace of 0.6 m/s. A physical examination reveals decreased grip strength and difficulty performing a chair stand test (rising from an armchair without using arms). Dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry (DEXA) scan shows a markedly reduced appendicular skeletal muscle mass index (ASMI) of 5.2 kg/m², below the threshold for sarcopenia (< 5.5 kg/m² in women). A muscle biopsy from the quadriceps femoris reveals evidence of motor unit loss, increased intermuscular fat deposition, and significant atrophy, particularly affecting fibers with larger diameters and faster contraction speeds. Immunohistochemical staining shows a relative decrease in the proportion of myosin heavy chain IIa (MHC IIa) and IIx (MHC IIx) positive fibers compared to myosin heavy chain I (MHC I) positive fibers. Which muscle fiber type is preferentially lost and atrophied in this patient's condition?

(A) Type I (slow-twitch) muscle fibers
(B) Type IIa (fast-twitch oxidative-glycolytic) muscle fibers
(C) Type IIx (fast-twitch glycolytic) muscle fibers
(D) All muscle fiber types are equally affected.
(E) Type III (intermediate-fast twitch oxidative) muscle fibers

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 82-year-old female, indicating advanced age, a primary risk factor for sarcopenia.
- **Symptoms:** Progressive weakness (especially stairs/rising), slowed gait (0.6 m/s), difficulty with chair stand – classic functional impairments associated with sarcopenia, reflecting loss of strength and power.
- **DEXA Findings:** Low ASMI (5.2 kg/m²) confirms reduced muscle mass, meeting diagnostic criteria for sarcopenia.
- **Muscle Biopsy Findings:** Motor unit loss (denervation), increased intermuscular fat (myosteatosis), significant atrophy, and preferential atrophy of larger, faster fibers. Immunohistochemistry shows decreased MHC IIa/IIx and relative preservation of MHC I fibers.
- **Question Stem:** Asks specifically which muscle fiber type is preferentially lost/atrophied.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
Sarcopenia is characterized by age-related loss of muscle mass, strength, and function. A key feature is the selective vulnerability of different muscle fiber types.
- **Muscle Fiber Types:** Skeletal muscle fibers are broadly classified into Type I (slow-twitch, oxidative) and Type II (fast-twitch). Type II fibers are further subdivided into Type IIa (fast-twitch oxidative-glycolytic) and Type IIx (fast-twitch glycolytic). Type I fibers are smaller, fatigue-resistant, rely primarily on oxidative metabolism, and are innervated by slow motor neurons. Type II fibers are larger, generate more force and power quickly but fatigue faster, rely more on glycolysis (especially IIx), and are innervated by faster motor neurons.
- **Pathophysiology of Sarcopenia:** Aging leads to several changes:
    - **Motor Neuron Loss:** Fast motor neurons, which innervate Type II fibers, are more susceptible to age-related degeneration and denervation than slow motor neurons innervating Type I fibers. This preferential denervation leads to atrophy of the corresponding Type II muscle fibers.
    - **Motor Unit Remodeling:** Surviving slow motor neurons sprout collateral branches to re-innervate orphaned Type II fibers. This process, called collateral re-innervation, leads to the formation of hybrid fibers (expressing characteristics of both fiber types) and fiber-type grouping (clusters of fibers of the same type innervated by the same motor neuron). While it partially compensates for motor unit loss, it doesn't fully prevent the overall decline in muscle mass and strength, especially power derived from Type II fibers.
    - **Anabolic Resistance:** Older muscles become less responsive to anabolic stimuli like protein intake and resistance exercise, further contributing to muscle loss.
    - **Inflammation:** Chronic low-grade inflammation ('inflammaging') may contribute to muscle protein breakdown.
- **Fiber Type Specificity:** Studies consistently show that Type II fibers, particularly Type IIx, are preferentially lost or atrophied in sarcopenia and other age-related muscle wasting conditions. This loss contributes significantly to the decline in muscle power and the ability to perform rapid, forceful movements. Type I fibers are relatively preserved, contributing to the maintenance of postural stability and endurance activities, although they also undergo some atrophy. The biopsy findings in the vignette (atrophy of larger, faster fibers; decreased MHC IIa/IIx relative to MHC I) directly support this preferential loss of Type II fibers.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Type I (slow-twitch) muscle fibers: Incorrect. Type I fibers are relatively *spared* in sarcopenia compared to Type II fibers due to the greater vulnerability of fast motor neurons and potentially greater resistance to age-related atrophy mechanisms. The vignette explicitly mentions preservation of MHC I fibers.
- (C) Type IIx (fast-twitch glycolytic) muscle fibers: Incorrect. While Type IIx fibers are indeed preferentially lost in sarcopenia, the question asks which *type* is preferentially lost. Type IIa fibers are also fast-twitch and are significantly affected, often more so than Type IIx in some studies, and are grouped together as Type II fibers in many contexts. Option (B) encompasses both major fast-twitch subtypes. Furthermore, Type IIx fibers are often converted to Type IIa fibers during aging or inactivity, so their absolute numbers might decrease, but the overall loss of *fast-twitch* fibers (Type II) is the key finding. Option (B) is a broader, more accurate description of the primary fiber type affected.
- (D) All muscle fiber types are equally affected: Incorrect. This contradicts the well-established finding of selective Type II fiber atrophy in sarcopenia, supported by the patient's biopsy results showing preferential atrophy of larger, faster fibers and decreased MHC II staining.
- (E) Type III (intermediate-fast twitch oxidative) muscle fibers: Incorrect. Type III fibers are not a standard classification in human skeletal muscle. The primary classification is Type I (slow) and Type II (fast), with Type II subdivided into IIa and IIx. While some intermediate phenotypes exist, they are not typically referred to as Type III in this context, and the core pathology involves the loss of the fast-twitch (Type II) population.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Sarcopenia is characterized by preferential atrophy of Type II (fast-twitch) muscle fibers, leading to decreased strength and power. This contrasts with conditions like disuse atrophy or spinal cord injury, where all fiber types may atrophy, or certain myopathies that selectively affect specific fiber types (e.g., mitochondrial myopathies often affect Type I fibers). Understanding the selective vulnerability of Type II fibers is crucial for understanding the functional decline in older adults.

<a id='question-081'></a>

### Question 081: Satellite Cell Activation and Muscle Regeneration
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Satellite cells are quiescent stem cells responsible for skeletal muscle regeneration after injury, characterized by the formation of new fibers with centrally located nuclei. (卫星细胞是骨骼肌损伤后负责再生修复的静止干细胞，其特征是形成具有中心核的新肌纤维。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male marathon runner experiences a significant hamstring strain during a race. Initial evaluation reveals severe pain and limited range of motion in the affected leg. Three weeks post-injury, a muscle biopsy is performed on the injured hamstring muscle. Histological examination of the biopsy specimen shows areas of myofiber necrosis interspersed with regions containing chains of small, basophilic cells arranged in parallel. These cells are multinucleated and exhibit prominent nuclei located centrally within the cytoplasm, rather than peripherally beneath the sarcolemma. Immunohistochemical staining confirms the presence of MyoD and myogenin expression in these regenerating cells.

Which of the following histopathological features is the most definitive indicator of ongoing skeletal muscle regeneration in this biopsy?

(A) Increased expression of dystrophin at the sarcolemma of existing muscle fibers.
(B) Presence of large, hypertrophied muscle fibers with peripheral nuclei.
(C) Formation of new blood vessels (angiogenesis) within the injured muscle tissue.
(D) Presence of centrally located nuclei within multinucleated myotubes.
(E) Increased deposition of collagen and fibrotic tissue replacing necrotic myofibers.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old male marathon runner (young, active individual).
- **Injury:** Hamstring strain (muscle injury).
- **Time Course:** Biopsy performed 3 weeks post-injury (allows time for regeneration to occur).
- **Histopathology:**
    - Areas of myofiber necrosis (initial injury).
    - Chains of small, basophilic, multinucleated cells (myotubes).
    - Nuclei located centrally within the cytoplasm (key feature).
    - Immunohistochemistry: MyoD and myogenin expression (markers of myoblast differentiation).
- **Question:** Asks for the *most definitive* indicator of regeneration.

The key clue is the description of "chains of small, basophilic multinucleated myotubes with prominent central nuclei." This directly points to the process of muscle regeneration mediated by satellite cells.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
Skeletal muscle possesses a remarkable capacity for regeneration following injury. This process is primarily driven by quiescent muscle stem cells known as satellite cells. These cells reside between the basal lamina and the sarcolemma of mature muscle fibers and are characterized by the expression of the transcription factor Pax7.

Upon muscle injury (e.g., contusion, strain, laceration), damaged muscle fibers release signaling molecules such as Hepatocyte Growth Factor (HGF), Fibroblast Growth Factor (FGF), and Insulin-like Growth Factor 1 (IGF-1). These factors activate the quiescent satellite cells.

Activated satellite cells undergo a series of events:
1.  **Activation & Proliferation:** Satellite cells withdraw from quiescence, become activated, and begin to proliferate, expanding their population.
2.  **Differentiation:** Proliferating satellite cells (now called myoblasts) differentiate into post-mitotic myotubes. This differentiation is regulated by key transcription factors, including MyoD (Myogenic differentiation factor) and myogenin. The vignette confirms the presence of MyoD and myogenin.
3.  **Fusion:** Myoblasts either fuse with existing damaged muscle fibers to contribute to their repair or fuse with each other to form new, multinucleated myotubes.
4.  **Maturation:** These newly formed myotubes mature into functional muscle fibers.

A hallmark feature distinguishing regenerating muscle fibers from mature, healthy fibers is the **location of the nuclei**. In mature skeletal muscle fibers, the nuclei are typically located peripherally, just beneath the sarcolemma. During regeneration, as myoblasts fuse to form new myotubes, the nuclei are initially located centrally within the cytoplasm. As the myotubes mature, the nuclei gradually migrate towards the periphery. Therefore, the presence of centrally located nuclei within multinucleated myotubes is the most characteristic and definitive histopathological sign of ongoing muscle regeneration.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Increased expression of dystrophin at the sarcolemma of existing muscle fibers. Dystrophin is a crucial protein linking the cytoskeleton to the extracellular matrix, essential for muscle fiber integrity. While dystrophin expression is vital for muscle function and repair, increased expression at the sarcolemma is not a specific or definitive marker of *regeneration*. It's more related to maintaining fiber integrity or potentially upregulated in response to damage, but not uniquely indicative of the *regenerative process* itself (formation of new fibers). In fact, dystrophinopathies (like Duchenne muscular dystrophy) involve *lack* of dystrophin, leading to muscle degeneration, not regeneration.
- (B): Presence of large, hypertrophied muscle fibers with peripheral nuclei. Large, hypertrophied muscle fibers with peripheral nuclei are characteristic of *mature*, healthy muscle fibers, especially in response to chronic overload or exercise. This finding is the opposite of what is seen during regeneration, where fibers are initially small and have central nuclei. This option describes the normal state, not regeneration.
- (C): Formation of new blood vessels (angiogenesis) within the injured muscle tissue. Angiogenesis is an important part of the healing process following muscle injury, providing nutrients and oxygen to the regenerating tissue. However, angiogenesis is a general response to tissue injury and inflammation, not specific to muscle regeneration. It occurs in many types of wound healing and is not the *most definitive* marker for muscle fiber regeneration itself.
- (E): Increased deposition of collagen and fibrotic tissue replacing necrotic myofibers. Fibrosis is a common outcome of muscle injury, particularly if regeneration is incomplete or impaired. Scar tissue (collagen) replaces the necrotic muscle fibers, leading to functional impairment. While fibrosis occurs alongside regeneration attempts, it represents a failure of complete regeneration or a late-stage response, not the active process of forming new muscle fibers. The vignette describes active regeneration (myotube formation), not primarily fibrosis.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The definitive histopathological hallmark of skeletal muscle regeneration is the presence of centrally located nuclei within newly formed multinucleated myotubes. This distinguishes regenerating fibers from mature fibers, which have peripheral nuclei. Satellite cells are the key players in this process. Differential diagnosis includes distinguishing regeneration from fibrosis (scar tissue) or simply mature muscle fibers. Understanding the role of satellite cells, MyoD/myogenin, and the characteristic nuclear location is crucial for USMLE Step 1.

---

<a id='question-082'></a>

### Question 082: Muscle Physiology - Length-Tension Relationship
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The length-tension relationship in skeletal muscle reflects the optimal overlap between actin and myosin filaments for cross-bridge formation, which directly determines the active force generated during contraction. (肌肉的长度-张力关系反映了肌动蛋白和肌球蛋白丝的最佳重叠度，从而决定了收缩过程中产生的有源力量。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A graduate student in muscle physiology is conducting an experiment using isolated frog sartorius muscle fibers. The fibers are mounted horizontally, and one end is fixed. The other end is attached to a force transducer. The muscle fiber is stimulated electrically, and the force generated is measured isometrically at various fixed lengths. The sarcomere length is precisely controlled and measured using laser diffraction microscopy. The student observes that the active force generated increases as the sarcomere length increases from approximately 1.6 µm to 2.2 µm, reaches a peak force around 2.2 µm, and then decreases as the sarcomere length increases further to 2.6 µm and beyond. The passive tension, measured by stretching the muscle fiber beyond its resting length, increases exponentially with increasing sarcomere length. The student plots the total tension (active + passive) against sarcomere length, obtaining a characteristic bell-shaped curve. The question is: What cellular interaction is the primary determinant of the magnitude of the *active* tension observed at each sarcomere length during this isometric contraction?

(A) The rate of calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum via ryanodine receptors (RyR1).
(B) The binding affinity of troponin C for calcium ions.
(C) The number of interacting actin-myosin cross-bridges formed at that sarcomere length.
(D) The rate of ATP hydrolysis by the myosin ATPase head.
(E) The diffusion distance of calcium ions from the T-tubules to the myofibrils.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The vignette describes a classic experiment demonstrating the length-tension relationship in skeletal muscle.
- Isometric contraction means the muscle length is held constant while force is measured.
- The experiment measures *active* tension (force generated by cross-bridge cycling) and *passive* tension (force generated by stretching elastic elements like titin).
- The bell-shaped curve of total tension vs. sarcomere length is characteristic.
- The question specifically asks about the determinant of *active* tension.
- The key is understanding the molecular basis of the length-tension relationship.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- The length-tension relationship arises from the optimal overlap between the thick (myosin) and thin (actin) filaments within the sarcomere.
- **Sarcomere Structure:** A sarcomere is the functional unit of muscle contraction, spanning from Z-disc to Z-disc. It contains overlapping thick (myosin) and thin (actin) filaments.
- **Sliding Filament Theory:** Muscle contraction occurs when thin filaments slide past thick filaments. This sliding is powered by the cyclical formation and detachment of cross-bridges between myosin heads and actin filaments.
- **Cross-Bridge Cycling:** Myosin heads bind to actin, pull the thin filaments towards the center of the sarcomere (power stroke), detach, and re-cock. This cycle requires ATP and calcium.
- **Length-Tension Relationship Explained:**
    - **Short Sarcomere Length (e.g., < 1.6 µm):** Thin filaments overlap significantly in the center of the sarcomere (H-zone is short or absent). This overlap hinders the movement of actin filaments during the power stroke, reducing the number of effective cross-bridges and thus active force.
    - **Optimal Sarcomere Length (e.g., ~2.2 µm):** There is maximal overlap between actin and myosin filaments, allowing for the maximum number of cross-bridges to form and cycle effectively. This results in the peak active force.
    - **Long Sarcomere Length (e.g., > 2.6 µm):** The overlap between actin and myosin filaments decreases. The thin filaments no longer extend into the center of the sarcomere (H-zone is wide). This reduces the number of potential cross-bridge interactions, decreasing active force.
- **Active Tension:** The force generated solely by the cross-bridge cycle. It is directly proportional to the number of cross-bridges that can form and cycle effectively at a given sarcomere length.
- **Passive Tension:** The force generated by the elastic recoil of the muscle's structural proteins (e.g., titin, collagen) when stretched. It increases exponentially with increasing sarcomere length.
- **Total Tension:** Active Tension + Passive Tension.
- Therefore, the number of interacting actin-myosin cross-bridges is the direct determinant of the active tension component of the length-tension relationship.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) The rate of calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) via ryanodine receptors (RyR1) primarily affects the *speed* and *magnitude* of the initial rise in active tension (calcium transient), but not the *peak* active tension determined by the length-tension relationship. While calcium is essential for cross-bridge cycling, the *number* of cross-bridges that *can* form is dictated by filament overlap, not the rate of calcium release itself. A faster calcium release might lead to a faster contraction, but the maximum force at a given length is still limited by the optimal overlap.
- (B) The binding affinity of troponin C for calcium ions determines the sensitivity of the contractile apparatus to calcium. Higher affinity means that lower calcium concentrations can trigger contraction. However, it doesn't determine the *maximum* active force possible at a specific sarcomere length. The maximum force is limited by the number of available cross-bridge sites due to filament overlap.
- (D) The rate of ATP hydrolysis by the myosin ATPase head determines the speed of the cross-bridge cycle (power stroke and detachment). A faster ATPase rate would lead to a faster contraction speed, but it doesn't directly determine the *magnitude* of the force generated at a specific sarcomere length. The force is determined by the number of cross-bridges cycling, which depends on overlap and calcium availability.
- (E) The diffusion distance of calcium ions from the T-tubules to the myofibrils affects the speed and uniformity of calcium delivery to the contractile proteins. A longer diffusion distance could potentially slow down the onset of contraction or lead to incomplete activation, but it does not fundamentally determine the maximum active force achievable at a given sarcomere length, which is governed by the actin-myosin overlap.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The length-tension relationship is a fundamental concept in muscle physiology, directly reflecting the optimal overlap of actin and myosin filaments for cross-bridge formation. Active tension is maximized when there is sufficient overlap for numerous cross-bridges to form and cycle effectively. This contrasts with passive tension, which arises from elastic elements and increases exponentially with stretch. Understanding this relationship is crucial for comprehending muscle mechanics and performance. Differentiate this from factors affecting contraction speed (calcium release rate, ATPase rate) or calcium sensitivity (troponin C affinity).

<a id='question-083'></a>

### Question 083: Sarcomere Length-Tension Relationship
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The optimal sarcomere length (L0) for maximal active isometric tension generation in skeletal muscle is determined by the degree of overlap between thick (myosin) and thin (actin) filaments, maximizing potential cross-bridge formation. (最佳肌节长度 (L0) 是产生最大主动等长张力时，肌动蛋白 (actin) 和肌球蛋白 (myosin) 丝之间的重叠度最佳，从而最大化潜在的横桥形成。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male athlete undergoes detailed muscle physiology testing as part of a performance enhancement study. During a series of experiments, researchers stimulate his isolated gastrocnemius muscle fibers and measure the active isometric tension generated at various resting sarcomere lengths using laser diffraction microscopy. The results show that the highest active isometric tension (defined as 100% of the maximum potential tension, P0) is consistently achieved when the sarcomere length is precisely maintained between 2.0 µm and 2.2 µm. At sarcomere lengths shorter than 2.0 µm, the active tension decreases significantly. At sarcomere lengths longer than 2.2 µm, the active tension also decreases, although less dramatically than at shorter lengths. The researchers note that the thick filaments (myosin) are approximately 1.65 µm long, and the thin filaments (actin) are approximately 1.0 µm long. The question is: What is the primary biophysical reason for the maximal active isometric force observed at a sarcomere length of 2.0 to 2.2 µm?

(A) At this length, the Z-discs are maximally spaced apart, allowing for the greatest range of thick filament movement within the sarcomere.
(B) At this length, the I-band is maximally wide, indicating minimal overlap between actin and myosin filaments, which reduces interference between cross-bridges.
(C) At this length, the overlap between the thick (myosin) filaments and the thin (actin) filaments is optimal, allowing the maximum number of potential cross-bridges to form.
(D) At this length, the A-band is maximally contracted, bringing the Z-discs closest together and maximizing the density of actin filaments.
(E) At this length, the calcium concentration within the sarcoplasm is highest, leading to maximal activation of troponin C and maximal binding of myosin heads to actin.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The vignette describes an experiment measuring active isometric tension at different sarcomere lengths.
- The key finding is maximal tension (100% P0) occurs between 2.0 µm and 2.2 µm.
- Tension decreases at lengths < 2.0 µm and > 2.2 µm.
- The lengths of thick (myosin, 1.65 µm) and thin (actin, 1.0 µm) filaments are provided.
- The question asks for the *primary biophysical reason* for this maximal tension.
- This points directly to the length-tension relationship and the concept of optimal overlap between actin and myosin.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- The length-tension relationship describes how the force a muscle fiber can generate depends on its length before stimulation.
- Active isometric tension is the force generated by the contractile elements (actin and myosin) when the muscle is stimulated but prevented from shortening.
- The maximal active tension (P0) occurs at the optimal sarcomere length (L0), typically around 2.0-2.2 µm in human skeletal muscle.
- **Mechanism:** The generation of force requires the interaction between myosin heads (on thick filaments) and actin binding sites (on thin filaments), forming cross-bridges.
    - **Overlap:** For cross-bridges to form, the thick and thin filaments must overlap.
    - **Optimal Overlap:** At L0 (2.0-2.2 µm), the overlap between the 1.65 µm myosin filaments and the 1.0 µm actin filaments is maximal. This allows the greatest number of myosin heads to potentially bind to actin and generate force simultaneously.
    - **Too Short (< L0):** If the sarcomere is too short (e.g., < 1.65 µm), the thin filaments from adjacent sarcomeres begin to overlap, and the Z-discs interfere with the movement of the thick filaments, reducing the number of potential cross-bridges and force. Also, the actin filaments within a single sarcomere may overlap excessively with the *same* myosin filament, reducing the number of available actin binding sites for *different* myosin heads.
    - **Too Long (> L0):** If the sarcomere is too long (e.g., > 2.2 µm), the overlap between thick and thin filaments decreases. The myosin heads may not be able to reach all the actin binding sites, reducing the number of cross-bridges formed and thus the force generated.
- Therefore, the optimal length is the point where the *number* of potential cross-bridges is maximized, leading to maximal force production upon stimulation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The Z-discs define the boundaries of the sarcomere. While their spacing changes with length, maximal spacing does *not* occur at L0. Maximal spacing occurs at longer lengths, where force is decreasing. The Z-discs do not directly limit thick filament movement in the way described; rather, overlap of thin filaments or lack of overlap with thick filaments limits force. Incorrect.
- (B): The I-band represents the region containing only thin filaments. At L0, the I-band is relatively narrow because the overlap between thick and thin filaments is maximal. A wide I-band indicates *minimal* overlap, which occurs at longer sarcomere lengths where force is decreasing. Incorrect.
- (D): The A-band represents the region containing thick filaments. The length of the A-band (1.65 µm) remains relatively constant regardless of sarcomere length because it corresponds to the length of the myosin filaments. The A-band does not "contract" significantly, and its length is not the primary determinant of optimal overlap. Incorrect.
- (E): Calcium concentration is crucial for initiating muscle contraction by binding to troponin C and allowing myosin heads to bind to actin. However, the *maximal* force generated at L0 is primarily determined by the *physical arrangement* (overlap) of the filaments, not the calcium concentration itself (assuming sufficient calcium is present for activation, which is the case in this isometric experiment). While calcium is necessary, it's not the *reason* for the optimal length effect. Incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- The optimal sarcomere length (L0) for maximal force generation is determined by the optimal overlap between actin and myosin filaments.
- This concept is central to understanding the length-tension relationship in skeletal muscle.
- Differentials: This mechanism differs from the sliding filament theory (which describes the *process* of contraction) and the excitation-contraction coupling (which describes the *initiation* of contraction by calcium). It specifically addresses the *length dependence* of force.

### Question 084: Skeletal Muscle Biophysics
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The ascending limb of the skeletal muscle length-tension curve (sarcomere length < 2.0 µm) is characterized by a decrease in active force due to steric hindrance and mechanical constraints. (骨骼肌长度-张力曲线的上升段 (肌节长度 < 2.0 µm) 的特点是由于空间位阻和机械约束导致主动力下降。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male marathon runner presents to the sports medicine clinic complaining of severe cramping in his calf muscles during prolonged exercise. Electromyography (EMG) shows normal motor unit recruitment patterns. A muscle biopsy is performed on the gastrocnemius muscle. In vitro experiments are conducted on isolated muscle fibers. When a single muscle fiber is stimulated to contract at an optimal sarcomere length (L0 = 2.2 µm), the active isometric tension generated is normalized to 100%. However, when the fiber is stretched to a sarcomere length of 1.6 µm prior to stimulation, the active isometric tension produced is only 40% of the maximal tension observed at L0. Further shortening the fiber to a sarcomere length of 1.2 µm results in an active isometric tension of only 15% of the maximal tension at L0. The passive tension component remains relatively low across all tested lengths. Which of the following mechanisms primarily explains the reduced active tension observed at sarcomere lengths below 2.0 µm?

(A) Decreased calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum due to reduced sensitivity of the ryanodine receptor (RyR1) to voltage changes at shorter lengths.
(B) Reduced actin-myosin cross-bridge cycling rate due to increased steric hindrance between opposing thin filaments and collision of thick filaments with Z-disks.
(C) Increased passive tension generated by the elastic elements of the sarcomere (titin, nebulin) at shorter lengths, opposing the active force generation.
(D) Decreased sensitivity of troponin C to calcium ions due to altered interfilament lattice spacing at shorter sarcomere lengths.
(E) Reduced ATP availability for myosin head activation and detachment due to increased metabolic demand at shorter sarcomere lengths.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient's symptoms (cramping) are less relevant to the specific biophysical question asked.
- The key information lies in the in vitro muscle fiber experiments.
- The experiment demonstrates the length-tension relationship.
- Maximal active tension is observed at L0 (2.2 µm).
- Active tension significantly decreases when the sarcomere length is shortened to 1.6 µm (40% of max) and further to 1.2 µm (15% of max).
- This decrease in tension at shorter lengths defines the ascending limb of the length-tension curve.
- The question asks for the *primary* mechanism causing this reduction in active tension.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- The length-tension relationship in skeletal muscle describes how the force generated by a muscle fiber varies with its length (specifically, sarcomere length).
- The curve has a descending limb (at very long lengths, > 2.2 µm), an optimal plateau (around L0), and an ascending limb (at short lengths, < 2.0 µm down to ~1.2 µm).
- The ascending limb is characterized by a progressive decrease in active force as the sarcomere shortens.
- The primary reasons for this decrease are mechanical constraints and steric interference:
    - **Thin Filament Overlap:** As the sarcomere shortens, the thin filaments (actin) from opposite sides of the sarcomere begin to overlap in the center. This overlap sterically hinders the movement of myosin heads and reduces the number of available binding sites for cross-bridge formation.
    - **Thick Filament-Z-Disk Collision:** At very short lengths (typically below 1.65 µm), the thick filaments (myosin) physically collide with the rigid Z-disks at the ends of the sarcomere. This collision impedes the sliding of thick filaments past thin filaments and restricts the overall shortening process, further reducing force generation.
- These mechanical factors directly impede the cross-bridge cycle (attachment, power stroke, detachment), leading to reduced active tension.
- Option (B) accurately describes these two primary mechanical limitations: opposing thin filament overlap and thick filament collision with Z-disks, which impede cross-bridge formation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Decreased calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR) due to reduced RyR1 sensitivity *can* occur at shorter lengths, but it is not considered the *primary* mechanism for the force reduction on the ascending limb. The main issue is mechanical interference with the cross-bridge cycle itself, not necessarily a lack of calcium triggering the cycle. While calcium sensitivity might be slightly affected, the mechanical constraints are dominant.
- (C) Increased passive tension *does* occur at shorter lengths due to the stretching of elastic elements like titin and nebulin. However, the question asks about the reduction in *active* tension. While passive tension increases, it doesn't explain the *decrease* in the force generated by the actin-myosin interaction itself. The passive tension opposes the active force, but it's not the cause of the active force reduction.
- (D) Decreased sensitivity of troponin C to calcium *can* occur at shorter lengths due to changes in the interfilament lattice spacing, which affects the conformational changes required for calcium binding and subsequent thin filament movement. However, this effect is generally considered secondary to the major mechanical constraints described in option (B). The steric hindrance and Z-disk collision are the more significant factors limiting force production on the ascending limb.
- (E) Reduced ATP availability is generally associated with fatigue or ischemia, not specifically with the length-dependent changes on the ascending limb of the length-tension curve under normal physiological conditions. While ATP is crucial for the cross-bridge cycle, its availability is not the primary limiting factor *specifically* at short sarcomere lengths in this context.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- The ascending limb of the length-tension curve (< 2.0 µm) reflects reduced active force due to mechanical interference: thin filament overlap and thick filament-Z-disk collision.
- Differentiate this from the descending limb (> 2.2 µm), where force decreases due to reduced overlap between thick and thin filaments.
- Understand that passive tension increases at both very short and very long lengths due to elastic element stretching.
- Recognize that calcium handling (RyR1 sensitivity, troponin C affinity) can be subtly affected by length but is not the primary driver of force reduction on the ascending limb.

<a id='question-085'></a>

### Question 085: Muscle Biophysics - Length-Tension Relationship
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The plateau region of the active length-tension curve (2.0-2.2 µm) is due to the central bare zone of the thick filament, which maintains a constant number of active cross-bridges. (主动力-长度曲线的平台区域 (2.0-2.2 µm) 是由于粗丝的中心裸区造成的，该区域保持了恒定的活性交叉桥数量。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
Dr. Anya Sharma, a muscle biophysicist, is conducting experiments on isolated psoas muscle fibers from a healthy adult volunteer. She uses a laser diffraction interferometer to precisely measure sarcomere length and a force transducer to measure isometric tension generated upon nerve stimulation. She systematically varies the sarcomere length from 1.6 µm to 2.4 µm while maintaining constant calcium concentration and ATP levels. Her results show that active isometric tension increases rapidly as sarcomere length increases from 1.6 µm to 2.0 µm. However, between sarcomere lengths of 2.05 µm and 2.18 µm, the active isometric tension remains remarkably constant and maximal, representing 100% of the maximum possible tension (P0). Dr. Sharma wants to understand the underlying structural basis for this observation. Which of the following molecular features of the sarcomere best explains why active isometric tension remains constant across this specific range of sarcomere lengths?

(A) The maximal overlap between actin and myosin filaments occurs at 2.1 µm, ensuring the highest possible number of cross-bridges are formed.
(B) The passive elastic elements (titin, nebulin) are maximally stretched at this length, providing maximal resistance to further shortening and maintaining tension.
(C) The central 0.2 µm region of the thick filament lacks myosin heads, preventing changes in the number of active cross-bridges as the thin filaments slide across this zone.
(D) The thin filaments are maximally aligned with the Z-discs at this length, optimizing the interaction between actin and tropomyosin-troponin complex.
(E) The sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium release channels (RyR1) exhibit maximal sensitivity to voltage changes at this sarcomere length, ensuring constant calcium availability for cross-bridge cycling.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient/Subject:** Healthy adult volunteer's psoas muscle fibers.
- **Experiment:** Measurement of active isometric tension vs. sarcomere length.
- **Key Finding:** Active isometric tension is constant and maximal between 2.05 µm and 2.18 µm (the plateau region).
- **Question:** What molecular feature explains this constant tension in the plateau region?

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
The active length-tension relationship describes how the force generated by a muscle fiber varies with its sarcomere length. This relationship is not linear; it exhibits a bell-shaped curve.
- **Descending Limb (Short Lengths < 1.8 µm):** At very short lengths, the thin filaments (actin) overlap significantly with the terminal ends of the thick filaments (myosin), which contain the S1 heads. This overlap interferes with cross-bridge formation, reducing active force.
- **Ascending Limb (1.8 µm to ~2.0 µm):** As sarcomere length increases, the overlap between actin and the myosin heads increases, leading to a rapid increase in the number of potential cross-bridges and thus active force.
- **Plateau (~2.0 µm to 2.2 µm):** In this narrow range, the thin filaments overlap maximally with the *central* region of the thick filament. Crucially, the central ~0.2 µm of the thick filament is composed of myosin molecules arranged tail-to-tail, lacking the S1 heads (the "bare zone" or "pseudo-H zone"). As the thin filaments slide across this bare zone during activation, the number of cross-bridges formed remains constant because there are no myosin heads available to bind to actin. This results in a constant, maximal active force.
- **Descending Limb (Long Lengths > 2.2 µm):** As sarcomere length increases further, the thin filaments begin to overlap with the terminal ends of the thick filaments again, reducing the number of available cross-bridges and decreasing active force.

The experiment described shows the characteristic plateau region (2.05 µm to 2.18 µm) where tension is maximal and constant. This phenomenon is directly explained by the presence of the central bare zone on the thick filament.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maximal overlap occurs around 2.1 µm, but this is *not* the reason for the *plateau* (constant tension). The plateau exists because the number of cross-bridges *doesn't change* over a small range, despite the thin filaments moving across the thick filament. Maximal overlap maximizes *potential* cross-bridges, but the bare zone limits the *actual* number formed within the plateau range. This option confuses maximal overlap with the mechanism of the plateau.
- (B): Passive elastic elements (like titin) contribute to passive tension and the overall length-tension curve shape, especially at longer lengths, but they do not explain the *constant active* tension within the plateau region. Active tension is generated by cross-bridges, not passive elements.
- (D): Thin filament alignment with Z-discs is important for overall sarcomere structure and function, but it doesn't specifically explain why tension remains constant *within* the 2.0-2.2 µm range. Changes in alignment would affect force, but the bare zone is the primary reason for the plateau's constancy.
- (E): Sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium release is crucial for initiating muscle contraction (activating cross-bridges), but its sensitivity doesn't explain the structural basis for the length-tension plateau. The plateau is a property of the sarcomere's architecture (thick filament structure), not the calcium release mechanism itself.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The plateau region of the active length-tension curve (2.0-2.2 µm) is a unique feature explained by the central bare zone of the thick filament. This zone lacks myosin heads, ensuring a constant number of active cross-bridges as thin filaments slide across it. This contrasts with the ascending and descending limbs, where changes in overlap affect the number of cross-bridges and thus active force. Understanding this structural basis is key to understanding muscle biophysics. Differential: The descending limb at short lengths is due to interference from terminal myosin heads, while the descending limb at long lengths is due to reduced overlap. The plateau is unique due to the bare zone.

<a id='question-086'></a>

### Question 086: Muscle Biophysics - Length-Tension Relationship
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The descending limb of the active length-tension curve in skeletal muscle is due to reduced overlap between actin (thin filaments) and myosin (thick filaments) as sarcomere length increases beyond the optimal length (approximately 2.2 µm), leading to decreased cross-bridge formation and active force generation. (肌肉长度-张力曲线的下降段是由于肌节长度超过最佳长度（约 2.2 µm）后，肌动蛋白（薄丝）和肌球蛋白（粗丝）之间的物理重叠减少，导致横桥形成减少和主动力降低。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A researcher is investigating the biophysical properties of isolated rabbit psoas muscle myofibrils. The myofibrils are mounted in a force transducer and stimulated electrically to contract isometrically. The sarcomere length is precisely controlled and measured using laser diffraction microscopy. The experimenter progressively stretches the myofibril from a resting sarcomere length of 2.2 µm to 2.8 µm in increments of 0.2 µm. At each length, the myofibril is stimulated, and the peak active isometric tension generated is recorded. The results show that as the sarcomere length increases from 2.2 µm to 2.8 µm, the active tension decreases linearly. The passive tension, measured at the same sarcomere lengths, remains relatively constant and low. The researcher observes that at 2.2 µm, the overlap between thick and thin filaments is maximal, allowing for the greatest number of cross-bridges to form. At 2.8 µm, the thin filaments are significantly pulled out of the A band, reducing the region where actin and myosin can interact.

What is the primary biophysical mechanism responsible for the observed linear decrease in active isometric tension as the sarcomere length increases from 2.2 µm to 2.8 µm on the descending limb of the length-tension curve?

(A) Increased steric hindrance between the Z-discs as the sarcomere stretches, preventing optimal thin filament alignment.
(B) Decreased availability of calcium ions within the sarcoplasmic reticulum, leading to reduced troponin C binding and actin-myosin interaction.
(C) Reduced number of myosin heads available on the thick filaments due to stretching-induced conformational changes.
(D) Progressive reduction in the physical overlap between the thick (myosin) and thin (actin) filaments, limiting the number of potential cross-bridges that can form.
(E) Increased passive tension generated by the stretching of elastic elements within the sarcomere, opposing the active force generated by cross-bridges.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Isolated myofibril:** This setup allows for the study of intrinsic muscle properties without interference from extracellular matrix or neural control.
- **Progressive stretch from 2.2 µm to 2.8 µm:** This range corresponds to the descending limb of the active length-tension curve. 2.2 µm is near the optimal length for maximal overlap.
- **Isometric stimulation:** Measures the force generated at a constant sarcomere length.
- **Linear decrease in active tension:** This is the key observation. The question asks for the *mechanism* causing this specific phenomenon.
- **Observation at 2.2 µm (maximal overlap):** Establishes the baseline condition for optimal force.
- **Observation at 2.8 µm (thin filaments pulled out of A band):** Describes the structural change occurring at longer lengths.
- **Constant low passive tension:** Rules out passive tension as the primary cause of the force decrease.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
The length-tension relationship describes how the active force generated by a muscle fiber (or myofibril) varies with its length. The curve has an optimal length (around 2.2 µm for skeletal muscle) where maximal active tension is produced.
- **Ascending Limb (Sarcomere length < 2.2 µm):** As the sarcomere shortens, the thin filaments move further into the A band, increasing the overlap with the thick filaments. This increases the number of potential cross-bridges that can form, leading to increased active tension. However, very short lengths (< ~2.0 µm) lead to interference between thin filaments and the Z-discs, reducing the number of available actin binding sites and decreasing tension.
- **Plateau (Optimal Length ~2.2 µm):** At this length, the overlap between actin and myosin is maximal, allowing for the maximum number of cross-bridges to form simultaneously. This results in the highest active tension.
- **Descending Limb (Sarcomere length > 2.2 µm up to ~3.65 µm):** As the sarcomere is stretched beyond the optimal length, the thin filaments are progressively pulled out of the A band. This reduces the region of overlap between actin and myosin. Consequently, fewer myosin heads can bind to actin, leading to a decrease in the number of active cross-bridges formed and a linear decrease in active tension. The relationship is approximately linear because the reduction in overlap is roughly proportional to the increase in sarcomere length within this range.
- **Beyond ~3.65 µm:** The thin filaments are so far out of the A band that there is virtually no overlap, and active tension drops to zero.

The question specifically asks about the *descending limb* (2.2 µm to 2.8 µm) and the *linear decrease* in active tension. This decrease is directly caused by the reduction in the physical overlap between the actin and myosin filaments, which limits the number of cross-bridges that can form.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Increased steric hindrance between Z-discs occurs at *shorter* sarcomere lengths (< 2.0 µm), contributing to the *ascending* limb's decline, not the descending limb. The Z-discs are not the primary limiting factor for overlap reduction on the descending limb.
- (B): Calcium availability is crucial for muscle contraction, but the experiment uses isometric stimulation, implying sufficient calcium is present for activation. The *linear decrease* in tension on the descending limb is a structural/mechanical phenomenon related to filament overlap, not primarily a calcium-dependent regulatory issue. While calcium levels *can* affect tension, they don't explain the specific length-dependent decrease observed here.
- (C): The number of myosin heads on the thick filament is relatively constant. Stretching does not significantly reduce the *number* of myosin heads available, although it does reduce the *accessibility* of those heads to actin binding sites due to reduced overlap. The primary issue is the reduced overlap, not a change in the number of heads.
- (E): Passive tension *does* increase as the sarcomere is stretched (due to elastic elements like titin and connective tissue), but the vignette states passive tension remains relatively constant and low. More importantly, the question asks for the mechanism of the *active* tension decrease, not passive tension increase. The linear decrease in *active* tension is specifically due to reduced cross-bridge formation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The descending limb of the active length-tension curve is a fundamental concept in muscle physiology. It demonstrates that muscle force is highly dependent on the precise alignment and overlap of actin and myosin filaments. Understanding this relationship is crucial for comprehending muscle function and dysfunction. Key differentials include understanding the ascending limb (Z-disc interference, increasing overlap) and the plateau (optimal overlap). This concept is distinct from the sliding filament theory itself, which describes the mechanism of contraction, and the excitation-contraction coupling, which describes the initiation of contraction.

<a id='question-087'></a>

### Question 087: Skeletal Muscle Biophysics - Length-Tension Relationship & Filament Overlap
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The length-tension relationship in skeletal muscle dictates that active force generation is maximal when thick and thin filaments optimally overlap (around 80% of sarcomere length, ~2.0-2.2 µm). At very long sarcomere lengths (≥ 3.65 µm), the thin filaments are pulled completely out of the A band, resulting in zero overlap and thus zero active force generation. (骨骼肌的长度-张力关系决定了当粗细丝和薄丝最佳重叠时（约占肌节长度的 80%，~2.0-2.2 µm），主动力生成最大。在非常长的肌节长度（≥ 3.65 µm）时，薄丝被完全拉出 A 带，导致重叠为零，从而主动力生成为零。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A researcher is studying the contractile properties of a single, isolated fast-twitch skeletal muscle fiber from a healthy adult human. The fiber is mounted on a high-precision force transducer and a micromanipulator capable of precisely controlling sarcomere length. The fiber is stimulated with a supramaximal electrical pulse, and the resulting active force is measured. The experiment is repeated while incrementally increasing the sarcomere length from 1.5 µm to 4.0 µm in 0.1 µm increments. At a sarcomere length of 3.65 µm, the force transducer registers 0 mN of active force, despite the presence of significant passive resistance (measured as 15 mN). The researcher then shortens the sarcomere to 2.1 µm, where the active force reaches a maximum of 150 mN upon stimulation. What is the primary biophysical reason for the complete absence of active force generation at a sarcomere length of 3.65 µm?

(A) Maximal activation of the thin filaments prevents cross-bridge formation with the thick filaments.
(B) The thick filaments are completely stretched and unable to bind to actin.
(C) The thin filaments are completely pulled out of the A band, preventing any overlap with the thick filaments.
(D) The Z-discs have fused together, limiting the extent of sarcomere shortening and preventing cross-bridge cycling.
(E) The calcium concentration in the sarcoplasm is insufficient to initiate cross-bridge formation at this sarcomere length.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Isolated skeletal muscle fiber:** Focuses the mechanism on intrinsic muscle properties, not systemic factors.
- **Force transducer:** Measures active and passive force.
- **Sarcomere length control:** Allows precise manipulation of the length-tension relationship.
- **3.65 µm length:** This specific length is the critical point where active force drops to zero.
- **0 mN active force, 15 mN passive resistance:** Confirms that the lack of force is *active* force, not total force. Passive force arises from elastic elements like titin.
- **2.1 µm length yields maximal active force:** Establishes the optimal sarcomere length for force generation.
- **Question asks for the *reason* for zero active force at 3.65 µm:** Requires understanding the molecular basis of the length-tension relationship.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
The active force generated by a sarcomere is directly dependent on the degree of overlap between the thick (myosin) and thin (actin) filaments. The cross-bridge cycle, where myosin heads bind to actin and generate force through conformational changes, requires this overlap.
- **Sarcomere Structure:** A sarcomere is the functional unit of muscle contraction, extending from Z-disc to Z-disc. It contains thick filaments (myosin, ~1.65 µm long) located in the center (A band) and thin filaments (actin, tropomyosin, troponin, ~1.0 µm long) anchored to the Z-discs. The A band is the region containing thick filaments and overlapping thin filaments (~2.2 µm long). The I band contains only thin filaments.
- **Length-Tension Relationship:**
    - **Short Sarcomeres (< ~1.8 µm):** Thin filaments from opposite sides overlap, interfering with cross-bridge cycling and reducing active force.
    - **Optimal Sarcomere Length (~2.0-2.2 µm):** Maximal overlap between thick and thin filaments allows for the most efficient cross-bridge cycling and maximal active force.
    - **Long Sarcomeres (> ~3.65 µm):** The thin filaments are pulled completely out of the A band. The thick filaments are now isolated, with no thin filaments available for cross-bridge binding. Consequently, no cross-bridges can form, and active force generation drops to zero. The measured tension at this length is solely passive tension due to the elastic properties of titin and other connective tissue elements.
- **Calculation:** The total length of the repeating unit (sarcomere) at the point of zero active tension is approximately the sum of the lengths of the thick filament and the two thin filaments: 1.65 µm (thick) + 1.0 µm (thin) + 1.0 µm (thin) = 3.65 µm.
- **In the vignette:** At 3.65 µm, the thin filaments are completely outside the A band, preventing any interaction with the thick filaments. Therefore, no cross-bridges can form, and active force is zero. The presence of passive force (15 mN) confirms that the muscle fiber is still capable of resisting stretch, but this force is not generated by the contractile machinery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maximal activation of thin filaments (e.g., by calcium binding to troponin C) is necessary for cross-bridge formation, not inhibitory. If thin filaments were maximally activated but unable to bind, it would imply a problem with myosin or cross-bridge cycling, not the fundamental length-dependent overlap. This is incorrect.
- (B): The thick filaments (myosin) are relatively inextensible. While they can be slightly stretched, they do not become completely unable to bind actin simply due to sarcomere length reaching 3.65 µm. The problem is the *absence* of thin filaments within binding range, not a failure of the thick filaments themselves. This is incorrect.
- (D): Z-disc fusion is characteristic of certain myopathies (e.g., nemaline myopathy) or occurs post-mortem, but it is not the physiological reason for zero active force at a specific sarcomere length in a healthy fiber. Z-disc fusion would impair sarcomere function generally, not specifically cause zero force only at 3.65 µm. This is incorrect.
- (E): Calcium is essential for initiating cross-bridge cycling by enabling the interaction between actin and myosin. However, the experiment involves maximal electrical stimulation, which ensures sufficient calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum. The absence of force at 3.65 µm is not due to lack of calcium but to the lack of physical overlap between filaments, regardless of calcium levels. This is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The length-tension relationship is fundamental to understanding muscle contraction. Remember that active force is zero when the sarcomere is stretched beyond approximately 3.65 µm because the thin filaments are pulled completely out of the A band, preventing overlap with the thick filaments. This contrasts with very short sarcomeres where thin filament overlap reduces force. Key differentials include understanding the roles of calcium, troponin/tropomyosin, and the cross-bridge cycle itself, but the primary determinant of force at extreme lengths is filament overlap.

<a id='question-088'></a>

### Question 088: Passive Tension in Skeletal Muscle
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Passive tension in skeletal muscle is the resistance to stretch in a relaxed muscle, primarily generated by the elastic properties of intracellular titin and extracellular connective tissue. (肌肉被动张力是指放松状态下肌肉抵抗被拉伸的力，主要由细胞内肌联蛋白和细胞外结缔组织的弹性特性产生。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male research subject is participating in a study investigating the mechanical properties of skeletal muscle. His right biceps brachii muscle is isolated and maintained in a state of complete relaxation, with no nerve stimulation and zero ATP consumption. The muscle is attached to a force transducer and a motor that can precisely control its length. Starting from a resting length (L0), the muscle is slowly stretched to increasing lengths. The force transducer records the following observations: At L0, the measured force is negligible (approximately 0 N). As the muscle length increases beyond L0, a progressive, exponential increase in force is measured, indicating significant resistance to stretch. Electron microscopy of a cross-section of the muscle fiber reveals abundant large elastic filaments spanning from the Z-disc to the M-line within the sarcomeres. Which of the following proteins is primarily responsible for generating this passive resting tension as the muscle is stretched beyond its optimal length?

(A) Actin
(B) Myosin
(C) Tropomyosin
(D) Titin (Connectin)
(E) Dystrophin

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Isolated, relaxed muscle:** The muscle is not stimulated electrically, and ATP consumption is zero, ruling out active tension generation via cross-bridge cycling (actin-myosin interaction). This points towards passive tension.
- **Stretched beyond L0:** The muscle is lengthened from its resting state.
- **Progressive, exponential increase in force:** As the muscle is stretched, resistance to stretch increases significantly. This is the hallmark of passive tension.
- **Electron microscopy:** Shows large elastic filaments spanning from Z-disc to M-line. This description perfectly matches the ultrastructure of titin.
- **Question asks for the primary intracellular generator:** The question specifically asks for the intracellular protein responsible.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
Passive tension is the force generated by a relaxed muscle when stretched beyond its resting length (L0). It is crucial for preventing overstretching and potential damage to the muscle fiber. Unlike active tension, which requires ATP and the interaction of actin and myosin filaments, passive tension arises from the elastic properties of the muscle's structural components.

The primary intracellular contributor to passive tension is **titin (connectin)**. Titin is a giant protein that spans half the sarcomere, extending from the Z-disc to the M-line. It acts like a molecular spring. In the relaxed state at L0, titin is relatively unstretched. As the sarcomere is stretched beyond L0, the I-band region of titin, containing numerous Ig-like domains and PEVK (Proline-Glutamic acid-Valine-Lysine) segments, begins to unfold and extend. This unfolding process generates an elastic restoring force that opposes further stretch. The force generated by titin increases exponentially with increasing stretch, matching the observations in the vignette.

Extracellular connective tissue (collagen fibers in the endomysium, perimysium, and epimysium) also contributes significantly to passive tension, especially at extreme lengths, providing high tensile strength. However, the question asks for the *primary intracellular* generator, which is titin.

Actin and myosin are the contractile proteins responsible for active tension generation through cross-bridge cycling. Tropomyosin regulates the interaction between actin and myosin. Dystrophin is a cytoskeletal protein linking the actin cytoskeleton to the extracellular matrix, important for muscle fiber integrity but not the primary generator of passive tension.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Actin**: Actin filaments are the thin filaments involved in cross-bridge formation with myosin during muscle contraction. They are essential for *active* tension generation, not passive tension in a relaxed muscle. Stretching actin filaments does contribute slightly to passive tension, but titin is the dominant intracellular contributor.
- (B) **Myosin**: Myosin filaments are the thick filaments that interact with actin to generate force during contraction. Like actin, myosin is primarily involved in *active* tension. Myosin's contribution to passive tension is minimal compared to titin.
- (C) **Tropomyosin**: Tropomyosin is a regulatory protein that wraps around the actin filament and blocks myosin-binding sites in the relaxed state. It plays a role in regulating muscle contraction but does not directly generate significant passive tension.
- (E) **Dystrophin**: Dystrophin links the internal actin cytoskeleton to the extracellular matrix via the dystrophin-glycoprotein complex. It provides structural support and helps transmit force but is not the primary protein responsible for the elastic recoil that generates passive tension when a muscle is stretched. Defects in dystrophin cause muscular dystrophy, characterized by muscle fiber fragility, not primarily altered passive tension properties (though secondary changes can occur).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Passive tension is the resistance to stretch in a relaxed muscle, generated by titin (intracellularly) and connective tissue (extracellularly). Titin acts as a molecular spring, unfolding as the muscle is stretched beyond its resting length, generating an exponential increase in force. This prevents overstretching and damage. Differentiate passive tension (no ATP, no cross-bridges) from active tension (ATP-dependent, cross-bridge cycling involving actin and myosin).
